        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-turn dialogue with Large Language Models (LLMs) suffers from progressive attention decay toward system prompt goal tokens. On Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) declines by 70.8\\% between early and mid-conversation turns, yet linear probes recover goal content from the residual stream with AUC\\,=\\,0.98. We introduce ThreadGate, a per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$) trained jointly with next-token prediction (NTP) and an auxiliary GAR loss. A five-way comparison reveals that training succeeds (teacher-forced GAR\\,=\\,0.50) but autoregressive GAR drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap; gating contributes zero measurable benefit and a PCGrad-based exclusion experiment rules out gradient conflict. The failure traces to exposure bias: attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text. Inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause consistent with our evidence. Scheduled sampling compresses the gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed), and cross-model evaluation on Llama-3.1-8B confirms the gap (27$\\times$ by median). These findings characterize how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary objective generalization under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses).\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0128L1oxNwatzVBRdLXFmpri`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Analysis}\label{sec:analysis}
2	
3	The five-way comparison in \S\ref{sec:experiments} established that gating produces no measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning. This section traces the failure through a three-step diagnostic chain: we first show that gates learn behavior opposite to the design intent (\S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}), then rule out gradient conflict as the explanation through an exclusion experiment (\S\ref{subsec:pcgrad}), and finally identify distribution shift as the root cause (\S\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms this selectivity with an orthogonal hidden-state metric, and \S\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms the failure generalizes across architecturally distinct LLMs.
4	
5	\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}
6	
7	The design intent of ThreadGate is to modulate the residual stream more aggressively at late conversation turns, where GAR naturally decays (\S\ref{sec:background}). We test whether the learned gates achieve this by comparing gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers of the trained model.
8	
9	The gates do the opposite. In 27 of 28 layers, the gate value at turn~1 is lower than at turn~21 (Wilcoxon signed-rank test: $p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$, effect size $r = 0.975$). At layer~23, the gate is 0.769 at turn~1 and 0.979 at turn~21; at layer~14, it is 0.747 at turn~1 and 0.928 at turn~21. The pattern is consistent: gates suppress the residual stream more at early turns (where GAR is already high) and become nearly passive at late turns (where intervention is most needed). This inversion has a straightforward explanation. NTP loss pressure is highest at late turns, where the model must predict tokens conditioned on a long context. The NTP gradient pushes gates toward 1.0 to preserve the full sublayer computation, and this pressure is strongest precisely where GAR-based attenuation would be most useful.
10	
11	The resulting inference-time gate values range from 0.93 to 0.98 (2--7\% attenuation), far below what meaningful modulation would require; this explains the convergence with LoRA-only in Table~\ref{tab:four-way}. Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}(a) visualizes the per-layer gate difference. The inverted turn conditioning raises a natural question: does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent gates from learning the intended behavior? If gradient conflict is the root cause, resolving it should rescue gate conditioning.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	  \centering
15	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}
16	  \caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift: the 20--33$\times$ GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base), the gap persists across all five tested configurations.}
17	  \label{fig:gate-shift}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\label{subsec:pcgrad}
21	
22	The NTP and GAR gradients point in opposing directions across 68--79\% of layers throughout training, with mean cosine similarity ranging from $-$0.089 (epoch~0) to $-$0.042 (epoch~2). The NTP gradient magnitude dominates by 7.5--14.7$\times$. This raises a natural hypothesis: perhaps opposing gradient directions prevent the GAR objective from converging. If so, eliminating direction conflict should prevent the collapse. We test this using PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects the gradient of one task onto the normal plane of the other whenever two task gradients conflict. We apply PCGrad to gate parameters only, scoping our claim to direction conflict; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium violations \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.
23	
24	Eliminating direction conflict via PCGrad shifts per-step cosine similarity from consistently negative ($-$0.089) to oscillating around zero ($-$0.15 to $+$0.09), yet all outcomes remain unchanged: IID GAR-ours (0.50 vs 0.50), PPL (2.780 vs 2.784), and autoregressive GAR (0.015 vs 0.016) are indistinguishable (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ follows a nearly identical training trajectory and collapses in the same manner (Figure~\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). The exclusion follows by \emph{modus tollens}: if direction conflict causes the failure, removing it should prevent it; PCGrad removes it; the failure persists; therefore direction conflict is not the cause. Direction conflict is neither necessary (removing it does not rescue GAR) nor sufficient ($\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ converges during training despite persistent conflict). The 7.5--14.7$\times$ NTP magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential. With gradient conflict ruled out, the question narrows: the training dynamics are not the problem (GAR converges under teacher forcing), so the failure must occur at the transition from training to inference.
25	
26	\begin{figure}[t]
27	  \centering
28	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}
29	  \caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID evaluation outcomes are indistinguishable: eliminating direction conflict changes nothing.}
30	  \label{fig:training-pcgrad}
31	\end{figure}
32	
33	\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\label{subsec:dist-shift}
34	
35	Gates are ineffective (\S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}) and gradient conflict is not the cause (\S\ref{subsec:pcgrad}). The TF-AR GAR gap (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}) persists across all training strategies. GAR-Dongre provides a revealing control: under standard training it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys the concentrated last-token attention pattern while leaving diffuse attention unaffected (scheduled sampling breaks this pattern; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
36	
37	The mechanism is exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled,arora2022exposure}: during training, the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. The statistical properties diverge, and the 2--7\% gate attenuation (\S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}) cannot compensate. \citet{he2019exposure} showed that language models exhibit token-level self-recovery from exposure bias, and our PPL results confirm this (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). The critical distinction is between primary and auxiliary objectives: NTP is robust because it is the same task at both training and inference time, while GAR optimizes a distributional property of the input prefix that has no counterpart under autoregressive generation. Figure~\ref{fig:attention-heatmap} confirms teacher-forced GAR-ours remains near $\tau = 0.5$ (7--8\% decay, $N$\,=\,45), yet autoregressive GAR collapses for all variants (Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}b).
38	
39	\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}
40	
41	\S\ref{subsec:dist-shift} showed that exposure bias selectively destroys GAR-ours while GAR-Dongre remains unaffected under standard training. We test whether this selectivity extends beyond attention with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between hidden states at the last-token position of adjacent assistant turns. For ThreadGate, TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 (gap $+$0.002); for LoRA-only, 0.349/0.411 (gap $-$0.062, reversed). Neither shows a gap comparable to the GAR collapse, confirming that exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior (summary-position attention to goal tokens) while leaving internal representations intact.
42	
43	Cross-model evaluation extends this selectivity: GAR-Dongre shows negligible distribution shift on both Llama-3.1-8B (AR/TF ratio 1.002$\times$) and Qwen-2.5-7B (1.28$\times$), compared to the order-of-magnitude collapse in GAR-ours (the scheduled sampling experiment in \S\ref{sec:discussion} breaks this pattern, with GAR-Dongre rising to 0.353).
44	
45	\subsection{Cross-Model Generality}\label{subsec:cross-model}
46	
47	To test whether the failure generalizes, we replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} with Base, ThreadGate, and LoRA-only. Phase~1 diagnostics confirm goal attention decay (GAR: 0.026$\to$0.003 over 10 turns). Teacher-forced GAR-ours ($N$\,=\,50) is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. We report median GAR-ours for autoregressive evaluation due to heavy-tailed distributions (5--8\% of turn-level measurements exhibit sporadic attention spikes). Median GAR-ours ($N$\,=\,20 mid-turn conversations, excluding the first two turns) is 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\times$ and 26$\times$. The TF-AR gap exceeds 25$\times$ on both architectures, consistent with the exposure bias hypothesis. The gate profile differs---Qwen's minimum at layers~3--4, Llama's at layer~25---yet both converge toward identity under NTP pressure.
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause (\S\ref{subsec:steering}). Alternative hypotheses (attention drift, KV cache accumulation, implicit label smoothing) remain open; our evidence is consistent with exposure bias as the dominant factor but does not exclude these alternatives. Split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring} complements $\beta$-steering by restructuring attention computation at the architectural level, but cannot resolve exposure bias in auxiliary losses requiring retraining.
4	
5	\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. AR GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the ${\sim}$31$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\ 1.36), consistent with exposure bias as a primary driver. GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\to$0.353). On the $N$\,=\,50 evaluation, $\beta$\,=\,5 incurs 24\% PPL cost (1.43\,$\to$\,1.77); $\beta$\,=\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (+9\%); scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining. A from-scratch second seed yields AR GAR\,=\,0.418 (gap 1.20$\times$), suggesting early scheduled sampling exposure may further compress the gap; systematic ablation is deferred.
6	
7	\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\beta$\,=\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; McNemar $p$\,=\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.
8	
9	TRC analysis (\S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without degrading representations. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.
10	
```

1	\section{Training Hyperparameters}\label{sec:appendix-hyper}
2	
3	All fine-tuned variants share the following configuration: Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct base model, LoRA rank 16 ($\alpha$=32) on \texttt{q\_proj} and \texttt{v\_proj}, AdamW optimizer (lr = $2 \times 10^{-4}$, $\beta_1$=0.9, $\beta_2$=0.999), batch size 1 with gradient accumulation 8, 3 epochs over 8K UltraChat conversations, maximum sequence length 2048, seed 42. ThreadGate gates use a 10$\times$ learning rate multiplier and initial bias 1.5 ($\sigma(1.5) \approx 0.82$).
4	
5	\section{Loss Formulation Search}\label{sec:appendix-loss}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:loss-iterations} summarizes the seven loss configurations explored before arriving at the final MSE + gate diversity formulation (\S\ref{sec:loss}). Configurations v1--v6 span three alternative loss families; none converged during training.
8	
9	\begin{table}[h]
10	\centering\small
11	\caption{Loss formulation iterations on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. GAR-ours (teacher-forced) and PPL are reported only for the converged configuration.}
12	\label{tab:loss-iterations}
13	\begin{tabular}{l l l c c}
14	\toprule
15	 & \textbf{Loss Family} & \textbf{Status} & \textbf{GAR} & \textbf{PPL} \\
16	\midrule
17	v1  & Cumulative            & \texttimes~GAR loss $\to$ 0     & ---  & ---  \\
18	v2  & Cumulative + $\varepsilon$ & \texttimes~same trajectory & ---  & ---  \\
19	v3  & Maximize ($\lambda$=2)  & \texttimes~gate frozen        & ---  & ---  \\
20	v3b & Maximize ($\lambda$=10) & \texttimes~identity collapse   & ---  & ---  \\
21	v5  & Maximize ($\lambda$=1)  & \texttimes~identity collapse   & ---  & ---  \\
22	v6  & Smooth saturation       & \texttimes~identity collapse   & ---  & ---  \\
23	v7  & MSE + gate div          & \checkmark~converged           & 0.50 & 2.78 \\
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}
26	\end{table}
27	
28	Maximize-family losses ($-\text{mean}(\text{GAR})$) consistently drove gates toward identity: the unbounded objective rewards full pass-through ($g \to 1.0$), collapsing gate diversity (std $<$ 0.025). Bounded variants (smooth saturation) decelerated but did not prevent this collapse, motivating the MSE formulation targeting a finite GAR level.
29	
```

      "content": "\\section{Training Hyperparameters}\\label{sec:appendix-hyper}\n\nAll fine-tuned variants share the following configuration: Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct base model, LoRA rank 16 ($\\alpha$=32) on \\texttt{q\\_proj} and \\texttt{v\\_proj}, AdamW optimizer (lr = $2 \\times 10^{-4}$, $\\beta_1$=0.9, $\\beta_2$=0.999), batch size 1 with gradient accumulation 8, 3 epochs over 8K UltraChat conversations, maximum sequence length 2048, seed 42. ThreadGate gates use a 10$\\times$ learning rate multiplier and initial bias 1.5 ($\\sigma(1.5) \\approx 0.82$).\n\n\\section{Loss Formulation Search}\\label{sec:appendix-loss}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:loss-iterations} summarizes the seven loss configurations explored before arriving at the final MSE + gate diversity formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). ```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	
6	We fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\texttt{q\_proj}, \texttt{v\_proj}). The combined loss $\mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \lambda_\text{GAR}\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} + \lambda_\text{div}\mathcal{L}_\text{div}$ uses $\lambda_\text{GAR} = \lambda_\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\tau = 0.5$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.
7	
8	We evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a learned constant vector (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning). \textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \S\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.
9	
10	\subsection{Main Results}
11	
12	Table~\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.
13	
14	The Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
15	
16	The teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\pm0.001$, gap ${\sim}$31$\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
17	
18	\begin{table*}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\,=\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\,pp spread); the 20--33$\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention}.}
22	\label{tab:four-way}
23	\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}
24	\toprule
25	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \textbf{AR} \\
26	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7} \cmidrule(lr){8-8}
27	\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\
28	\midrule
29	Base              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\
30	ThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\
31	\;+\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\
32	Uniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\
33	LoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\
34	\bottomrule
35	\end{tabular}
36	\end{table*}
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	  \centering
40	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}
41	  \caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\,=\,45, $\pm$1\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\tau$\,=\,0.5 with 7--8\% decay (T1$\to$T4); Base decays 80\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode.}
42	  \label{fig:attention-heatmap}
43	\end{figure}
44	
45	\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\label{subsec:steering}
46	
47	All training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.
48	
49	An initial sweep on $N$\,=\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\beta$\,=\,2 and $\beta$\,=\,5: GAR increases ${\sim}$11$\times$ (0.062\,$\to$\,0.6795$\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\% (1.41\,$\to$\,1.91). At $\beta$\,=\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.
50	
51	$N$\,=\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\,=\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\pm$0.110 ($\beta$\,=\,2) to 0.556$\pm$0.060 ($\beta$\,=\,3), an 8.7$\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\% PPL increase (1.43\,$\to$\,1.56), identifying $\beta$\,=\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\beta$\,=\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\,=\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\beta$\,=\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.
52	
53	These results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.
54	
55	\begin{figure}[t]
56	  \centering
57	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}
58	  \caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\pm$1\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\,=\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\beta \in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\sim}$11$\times$ recovery with 35\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\,=\,50, 39 with $\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\beta$\,=\,2 and $\beta$\,=\,3, identifying $\beta$\,=\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\times$ GAR recovery, 9\% PPL cost). High-$\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}
59	  \label{fig:steering}
60	\end{figure}
61	
62

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Multi-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.
4	
5	A striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \emph{not} affect. \citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\,=\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.
6	
7	\begin{figure*}[t]
8	  \centering
9	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
10	  \caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}
11	  \label{fig:overview}
12	\end{figure*}
13	
14	ThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\S\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way controlled comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one factor (\S\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.
15	
16	Training succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The five-way comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a gap exceeding an order of magnitude that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\S\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the ${\sim}$31$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
17	
18	Our contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\S\ref{sec:method}, \S\ref{sec:experiments}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\sim}$31$\times$ to 1.57$\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\beta$\,=\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \S\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline: the final MSE formulation emerges from six failed loss families, and the five-way comparison rules out four alternative training strategies (\S\ref{sec:method}, \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
```

    "newString": "\\textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a still-learnable constant vector, preserving all gate parameters except turn conditioning (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a learned constant vector (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nAn initial sweep on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; McNemar $p$\\,=\\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.\n\nTRC analysis",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). Alternative hypotheses (attention drift, KV cache accumulation, implicit label smoothing) remain open; our evidence is consistent with exposure bias as the dominant factor but does not exclude these alternatives. Split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} complements $\\beta$-steering by restructuring attention computation at the architectural level, but cannot resolve exposure bias in auxiliary losses requiring retraining.\n\n\\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. AR GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\\ 1.36), consistent with exposure bias as a primary driver. GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\\to$0.353). On the $N$\\,=\\,50 evaluation, $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 incurs 24\\% PPL cost (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.77); $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (+9\\%); scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining. A from-scratch second seed yields AR GAR\\,=\\,0.418 (gap 1.20$\\times$), suggesting early scheduled sampling exposure may further compress the gap; systematic ablation is deferred.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; McNemar $p$\\,=\\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.\n\nTRC analysis (\\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without degrading representations. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "93406338-d26d-4322-a9ef-9cd3789be1d3",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "e376aef1-3f9d-4b3d-b695-5fec26a67fe8",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery",
    "newString": "a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient direction conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \\citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \\citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.\n\nA striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \\emph{not} affect. \\citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\\,=\\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n  \\caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}\n  \\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way controlled comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one factor (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.\n\nTraining succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The five-way comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a gap exceeding an order of magnitude that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline: the final MSE formulation emerges from six failed loss families, and the five-way comparison rules out four alternative training strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "a4c5ab73-cc01-4d64-b500-400d7bad0751",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "e376aef1-3f9d-4b3d-b695-5fec26a67fe8",

  "uuid": "c05065cb-76b3-44b9-971d-1d6e76296a59",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "(b)~Distribution shift: the 20--33$\\times$ GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base), the gap persists across all five tested configurations.}",
    "newString": "(b)~Distribution shift: the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base) under our evaluation conditions.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison in \\S\\ref{sec:experiments} established that gating produces no measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning. This section traces the failure through a three-step diagnostic chain: we first show that gates learn behavior opposite to the design intent (\\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}), then rule out gradient conflict as the explanation through an exclusion experiment (\\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad}), and finally identify distribution shift as the root cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms this selectivity with an orthogonal hidden-state metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms the failure generalizes across architecturally distinct LLMs.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nThe design intent of ThreadGate is to modulate the residual stream more aggressively at late conversation turns, where GAR naturally decays (\\S\\ref{sec:background}). We test whether the learned gates achieve this by comparing gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers of the trained model.\n\nThe gates do the opposite. In 27 of 28 layers, the gate value at turn~1 is lower than at turn~21 (Wilcoxon signed-rank test: $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, effect size $r = 0.975$). At layer~23, the gate is 0.769 at turn~1 and 0.979 at turn~21; at layer~14, it is 0.747 at turn~1 and 0.928 at turn~21. The pattern is consistent: gates suppress the residual stream more at early turns (where GAR is already high) and become nearly passive at late turns (where intervention is most needed). This inversion has a straightforward explanation. NTP loss pressure is highest at late turns, where the model must predict tokens conditioned on a long context. The NTP gradient pushes gates toward 1.0 to preserve the full sublayer computation, and this pressure is strongest precisely where GAR-based attenuation would be most useful.\n\nThe resulting inference-time gate values range from 0.93 to 0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation), far below what meaningful modulation would require; this explains the convergence with LoRA-only in Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}. Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}(a) visualizes the per-layer gate difference. The inverted turn conditioning raises a natural question: does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent gates from learning the intended behavior? If gradient conflict is the root cause, resolving it should rescue gate conditioning.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift: the 20--33$\\times$ GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base), the gap persists across all five tested configurations.}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nThe NTP and GAR gradients point in opposing directions across 68--79\\% of layers throughout training, with mean cosine similarity ranging from $-$0.089 (epoch~0) to $-$0.042 (epoch~2). The NTP gradient magnitude dominates by 7.5--14.7$\\times$. This raises a natural hypothesis: perhaps opposing gradient directions prevent the GAR objective from converging. If so, eliminating direction conflict should prevent the collapse. We test this using PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects the gradient of one task onto the normal plane of the other whenever two task gradients conflict. We apply PCGrad to gate parameters only, scoping our claim to direction conflict; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium violations \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.\n\nEliminating direction conflict via PCGrad shifts per-step cosine similarity from consistently negative ($-$0.089) to oscillating around zero ($-$0.15 to $+$0.09), yet all outcomes remain unchanged: IID GAR-ours (0.50 vs 0.50), PPL (2.780 vs 2.784), and autoregressive GAR (0.015 vs 0.016) are indistinguishable (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ follows a nearly identical training trajectory and collapses in the same manner (Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). The exclusion follows by \\emph{modus tollens}: if direction conflict causes the failure, removing it should prevent it; PCGrad removes it; the failure persists; therefore direction conflict is not the cause. Direction conflict is neither necessary (removing it does not rescue GAR) nor sufficient ($\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ converges during training despite persistent conflict). The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ NTP magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential. With gradient conflict ruled out, the question narrows: the training dynamics are not the problem (GAR converges under teacher forcing), so the failure must occur at the transition from training to inference.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID evaluation outcomes are indistinguishable: eliminating direction conflict changes nothing.}\n  \\label{fig:training-pcgrad}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nGates are ineffective (\\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}) and gradient conflict is not the cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad}). The TF-AR GAR gap (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}) persists across all training strategies. GAR-Dongre provides a revealing control: under standard training it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys the concentrated last-token attention pattern while leaving diffuse attention unaffected (scheduled sampling breaks this pattern; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,arora2022exposure}: during training, the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. The statistical properties diverge, and the 2--7\\% gate attenuation (\\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}) cannot compensate. \\citet{he2019exposure} showed that language models exhibit token-level self-recovery from exposure bias, and our PPL results confirm this (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). The critical distinction is between primary and auxiliary objectives: NTP is robust because it is the same task at both training and inference time, while GAR optimizes a distributional property of the input prefix that has no counterpart under autoregressive generation. Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap} confirms teacher-forced GAR-ours remains near $\\tau = 0.5$ (7--8\\% decay, $N$\\,=\\,45), yet autoregressive GAR collapses for all variants (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}b).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\n\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} showed that exposure bias selectively destroys GAR-ours while GAR-Dongre remains unaffected under standard training. We test whether this selectivity extends beyond attention with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between hidden states at the last-token position of adjacent assistant turns. For ThreadGate, TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 (gap $+$0.002); for LoRA-only, 0.349/0.411 (gap $-$0.062, reversed). Neither shows a gap comparable to the GAR collapse, confirming that exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior (summary-position attention to goal tokens) while leaving internal representations intact.\n\nCross-model evaluation extends this selectivity: GAR-Dongre shows negligible distribution shift on both Llama-3.1-8B (AR/TF ratio 1.002$\\times$) and Qwen-2.5-7B (1.28$\\times$), compared to the order-of-magnitude collapse in GAR-ours (the scheduled sampling experiment in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion} breaks this pattern, with GAR-Dongre rising to 0.353).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Generality}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nTo test whether the failure generalizes, we replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} with Base, ThreadGate, and LoRA-only. Phase~1 diagnostics confirm goal attention decay (GAR: 0.026$\\to$0.003 over 10 turns). Teacher-forced GAR-ours ($N$\\,=\\,50) is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. We report median GAR-ours for autoregressive evaluation due to heavy-tailed distributions (5--8\\% of turn-level measurements exhibit sporadic attention spikes). Median GAR-ours ($N$\\,=\\,20 mid-turn conversations, excluding the first two turns) is 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$. The TF-AR gap exceeds 25$\\times$ on both architectures, consistent with the exposure bias hypothesis. The gate profile differs---Qwen's minimum at layers~3--4, Llama's at layer~25---yet both converge toward identity under NTP pressure.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a still-learnable constant vector, preserving all gate parameters except turn conditioning (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations under our evaluation conditions. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nAn initial sweep on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

    "oldString": "(2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization",
    "newString": "(2)~using this framework, to our knowledge the first empirical characterization",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \\citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \\citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.\n\nA striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \\emph{not} affect. \\citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\\,=\\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n  \\caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}\n  \\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way controlled comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one factor (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.\n\nTraining succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The five-way comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a gap exceeding an order of magnitude that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient direction conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline: the final MSE formulation emerges from six failed loss families, and the five-way comparison rules out four alternative training strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient direction conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, to our knowledge the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline: the final MSE formulation emerges from six failed loss families, and the five-way comparison rules out four alternative training strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
