    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a still-learnable constant vector, preserving all gate parameters except turn conditioning (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nAn initial sweep on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). Alternative hypotheses remain open; our evidence is consistent with exposure bias as the dominant factor but does not exclude alternatives. In particular, KV cache drift---the accumulation of model-generated key-value states whose statistics diverge from teacher-forced states---could compound the distributional mismatch beyond what prefix-level exposure bias alone predicts. Disentangling prefix-level and cache-level contributions requires interventions on cached representations, which we leave to future work. Split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} complements $\\beta$-steering by restructuring attention computation at the architectural level, but cannot resolve exposure bias in auxiliary losses requiring retraining.\n\n\\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\\to$0.353). On the $N$\\,=\\,50 evaluation, $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 incurs 24\\% PPL cost (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.77); $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (+9\\%); scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining. A from-scratch second seed yields AR GAR\\,=\\,0.418 (gap 1.20$\\times$), suggesting early scheduled sampling exposure may further compress the gap; systematic ablation is deferred.\n\nExploratory instruction retention analysis (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}) shows +23.8\\,pp content compliance at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, though statistical significance is marginal (McNemar $p$\\,=\\,0.077).\n\nTRC analysis (\\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without degrading representations. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.\n",

We note that this steering experiment is diagnostic, not a deployment strategy: it requires explicit identification of goal tokens, which is trivial for system prompts but does not generalize to implicit goals.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a still-learnable constant vector, preserving all gate parameters except turn conditioning (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations under our evaluation conditions. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode under these conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nAn initial sweep on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a still-learnable constant vector, preserving all gate parameters except turn conditioning (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations under our evaluation conditions. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode under these conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nAn initial sweep on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier. We note that this steering experiment is diagnostic, not a deployment strategy: it requires explicit identification of goal tokens, which is trivial for system prompts but does not generalize to implicit goals.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "82b5961e-5b63-4921-8821-10e69dbff4c7",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "e376aef1-3f9d-4b3d-b695-5fec26a67fe8",

  "uuid": "406e0108-79cb-4e17-b7c9-5c560c0d6dec",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "\\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream.",
    "newString": "\\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention; Rhea \\citep{hong2025rhea} introduces role-aware episodic attention for multi-turn dialogue. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn attention degradation.}\nRecent work has established that LLMs systematically degrade across conversation turns. \\citet{laban2025lost} showed that model performance drops on tasks placed in later turns, even when the tasks are identical to earlier ones. \\citet{liu2026intent} traced a related failure to intent mismatch, where models progressively shift from the user's original intent as dialogue extends. MT-Bench-101 \\citep{bai2024mtbench101} provides fine-grained multi-turn evaluation across 1388 turn-level instances, revealing that capability varies substantially across task categories. \\citet{dongre2026attention} established the quantitative foundation for this line of work: they defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) and showed that attention to system-prompt goal tokens decays by 27--48\\% across turns in 12 models, while residual stream probes retain goal information with AUC\\,=\\,0.98. We observe a steeper 70.8\\% decline on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). This dissociation between attention collapse and residual persistence motivates our gating intervention. Their work characterizes the phenomenon; ours attempts the first systematic cure and, in doing so, uncovers why lightweight interventions fail to transfer from training to inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction stability.}\n\\citet{li2024measuring} independently quantified instruction instability in multi-turn dialogs and proposed split-softmax---separating instruction from conversation tokens during attention computation---as an inference-time remedy. Split-softmax restructures the attention mechanism itself and requires no retraining, but cannot address exposure bias in auxiliary losses that depend on training-time optimization. Our work adds systematic diagnosis (five-way controlled comparison + PCGrad exclusion), exposure bias attribution, and training-time mitigation via scheduled sampling; our $\\beta$-steering complements split-softmax as an alternative inference-time approach (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Residual gating and adaptive depth.}\nReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero} showed that residual scaling shapes optimization dynamics. Subsequent work targets computational efficiency: LayerDrop \\citep{fan2020layerdrop}, Mixture-of-Depths \\citep{raposo2024mixture}, FlexiDepth \\citep{luo2025flexidepth}, and CALM \\citep{schuster2022calm}. ThreadGate applies residual gating to a different objective: recovering attention patterns in multi-turn dialogue.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention manipulation.}\nStreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} identified attention sinks and proposed anchor token retention; \\citet{hsieh2024found} showed positional bias against middle-positioned information; \\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream. Our results suggest that neither pathway alone overcomes the distributional gap between teacher-forced training and autoregressive inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-task gradient conflict.}\nGradNorm \\citep{chen2018gradnorm}, PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, CAGrad \\citep{liu2021cagrad}, and Nash-MTL \\citep{navon2022nash} treat gradient conflict between competing losses as a root cause to be resolved through better optimization. We repurpose PCGrad as a diagnostic tool: eliminating directional conflict between NTP and GAR gradients does not improve GAR convergence. Gradient conflict is a symptom rather than the cause.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure bias.}\nTeacher forcing creates a distributional mismatch between training and inference that compounds over sequence length \\citep{wiseman2016sequence,arora2022exposure}. Scheduled Sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled} introduced curriculum-based mixing of ground-truth and model-generated tokens. MIXER \\citep{ranzato2016sequence} combined token-level and sequence-level objectives, and Professor Forcing \\citep{lamb2016professor} used adversarial regularization to align hidden state distributions under both regimes. \\citet{he2019exposure} showed that autoregressive models exhibit self-recovery from exposure bias at the token prediction level. SEARNN \\citep{leblond2018searnn} proposed locally recurrent search-based training to avoid the mismatch entirely. This literature focuses on generation quality degradation in primary objectives. No prior work examines how exposure bias affects auxiliary objectives that target attention distributions, nor how it manifests in multi-turn dialogue settings. Our work identifies a distinct failure mode: auxiliary attention losses that converge under teacher forcing fail completely under autoregressive generation, even when NTP remains robust, because token-level self-recovery does not extend to distributional properties of attention.",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 10,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 10,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero} showed that residual scaling shapes optimization dynamics. Subsequent work targets computational efficiency: LayerDrop \\citep{fan2020layerdrop}, Mixture-of-Depths \\citep{raposo2024mixture}, FlexiDepth \\citep{luo2025flexidepth}, and CALM \\citep{schuster2022calm}. ThreadGate applies residual gating to a different objective: recovering attention patterns in multi-turn dialogue.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Attention manipulation.}",
          "-StreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} identified attention sinks and proposed anchor token retention; \\citet{hsieh2024found} showed positional bias against middle-positioned information; \\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream. Our results suggest that neither pathway alone overcomes the distributional gap between teacher-forced training and autoregressive inference.",
          "+StreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} identified attention sinks and proposed anchor token retention; \\citet{hsieh2024found} showed positional bias against middle-positioned information; \\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention; Rhea \\citep{hong2025rhea} introduces role-aware episodic attention for multi-turn dialogue. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream. Our results suggest that neither pathway alone overcomes the distributional gap between teacher-forced training and autoregressive inference.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Multi-task gradient conflict.}",
          " GradNorm \\citep{chen2018gradnorm}, PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, CAGrad \\citep{liu2021cagrad}, and Nash-MTL \\citep{navon2022nash} treat gradient conflict between competing losses as a root cause to be resolved through better optimization. We repurpose PCGrad as a diagnostic tool: eliminating directional conflict between NTP and GAR gradients does not improve GAR convergence. Gradient conflict is a symptom rather than the cause."
        ]
      }

Let me reconsider - maybe the Director wants the first several authors listed before "and others"? Like "Grattafiori, Aaron and Dubey, Abhimanyu and ... and others"? Let me look at how other bib entries handle this.

Based on the search results, I found information related to Guangxuan Xiao's 2024 work on efficient LLMs. Here's what I found:

Guangxuan Xiao submitted his thesis on May 17, 2024, titled "Efficient Deployment Algorithms for Large Language Models" to MIT's Department of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science. The work addresses how massive computational and memory requirements of LLMs hinder widespread deployment, and presents techniques to enable efficient and accurate quantization and streaming deployment of LLMs.

The thesis develops SmoothQuant, an accurate post-training 8-bit quantization method for both weights and activations in LLMs up to 530B parameters.

Additionally, in 2023, Xiao interned at Meta researching "Efficient Attention Mechanism for Streaming Language Models", with results published on arxiv. His key contributions, including SmoothQuant and StreamingLLM, have been widely adopted and integrated into platforms such as NVIDIA's TensorRT-LLM, HuggingFace, and Intel's Neural Compressor.

While the search results don't show a specific 2024 arxiv paper from Xiao specifically about "depth layers," the results do show his work on efficient LLM deployment and attention mechanisms. If you're looking for a specific arxiv paper about depth layers, you may want to check arxiv.org directly with a more specific paper ID or title.

    "newString": "A recurring theme is that simple depth interventions can be surprisingly effective: \\citet{gromov2024unreasonable} showed that removing up to half of a model's layers often preserves most capability, foreshadowing our finding that LoRA-only fine-tuning matches all gating variants. ThreadGate applies residual gating to a different objective: recovering attention patterns in multi-turn dialogue.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn attention degradation.}\nRecent work has established that LLMs systematically degrade across conversation turns. \\citet{laban2025lost} showed that model performance drops on tasks placed in later turns, even when the tasks are identical to earlier ones. \\citet{liu2026intent} traced a related failure to intent mismatch, where models progressively shift from the user's original intent as dialogue extends. MT-Bench-101 \\citep{bai2024mtbench101} provides fine-grained multi-turn evaluation across 1388 turn-level instances, revealing that capability varies substantially across task categories. \\citet{dongre2026attention} established the quantitative foundation for this line of work: they defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) and showed that attention to system-prompt goal tokens decays by 27--48\\% across turns in 12 models, while residual stream probes retain goal information with AUC\\,=\\,0.98. We observe a steeper 70.8\\% decline on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). This dissociation between attention collapse and residual persistence motivates our gating intervention. Their work characterizes the phenomenon; ours attempts the first systematic cure and, in doing so, uncovers why lightweight interventions fail to transfer from training to inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction stability.}\n\\citet{li2024measuring} independently quantified instruction instability in multi-turn dialogs and proposed split-softmax---separating instruction from conversation tokens during attention computation---as an inference-time remedy. Split-softmax restructures the attention mechanism itself and requires no retraining, but cannot address exposure bias in auxiliary losses that depend on training-time optimization. Our work adds systematic diagnosis (five-way controlled comparison + PCGrad exclusion), exposure bias attribution, and training-time mitigation via scheduled sampling; our $\\beta$-steering complements split-softmax as an alternative inference-time approach (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Residual gating and adaptive depth.}\nReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero} showed that residual scaling shapes optimization dynamics. Subsequent work targets computational efficiency: LayerDrop \\citep{fan2020layerdrop}, Mixture-of-Depths \\citep{raposo2024mixture}, FlexiDepth \\citep{luo2025flexidepth}, and CALM \\citep{schuster2022calm}. ThreadGate applies residual gating to a different objective: recovering attention patterns in multi-turn dialogue.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention manipulation.}\nStreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} identified attention sinks and proposed anchor token retention; \\citet{hsieh2024found} showed positional bias against middle-positioned information; \\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention; Rhea \\citep{hong2025rhea} introduces role-aware episodic attention for multi-turn dialogue. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream. Our results suggest that neither pathway alone overcomes the distributional gap between teacher-forced training and autoregressive inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-task gradient conflict.}\nGradNorm \\citep{chen2018gradnorm}, PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, CAGrad \\citep{liu2021cagrad}, and Nash-MTL \\citep{navon2022nash} treat gradient conflict between competing losses as a root cause to be resolved through better optimization. We repurpose PCGrad as a diagnostic tool: eliminating directional conflict between NTP and GAR gradients does not improve GAR convergence. Gradient conflict is a symptom rather than the cause.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure bias.}\nTeacher forcing creates a distributional mismatch between training and inference that compounds over sequence length \\citep{wiseman2016sequence,arora2022exposure}. Scheduled Sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled} introduced curriculum-based mixing of ground-truth and model-generated tokens. MIXER \\citep{ranzato2016sequence} combined token-level and sequence-level objectives, and Professor Forcing \\citep{lamb2016professor} used adversarial regularization to align hidden state distributions under both regimes. \\citet{he2019exposure} showed that autoregressive models exhibit self-recovery from exposure bias at the token prediction level. SEARNN \\citep{leblond2018searnn} proposed locally recurrent search-based training to avoid the mismatch entirely. This literature focuses on generation quality degradation in primary objectives. No prior work examines how exposure bias affects auxiliary objectives that target attention distributions, nor how it manifests in multi-turn dialogue settings. Our work identifies a distinct failure mode: auxiliary attention losses that converge under teacher forcing fail completely under autoregressive generation, even when NTP remains robust, because token-level self-recovery does not extend to distributional properties of attention.",

          " StreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} identified attention sinks and proposed anchor token retention; \\citet{hsieh2024found} showed positional bias against middle-positioned information; \\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention; Rhea \\citep{hong2025rhea} introduces role-aware episodic attention for multi-turn dialogue. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream. Our results suggest that neither pathway alone overcomes the distributional gap between teacher-forced training and autoregressive inference."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },

12	
13	## Story Types
14	
15	突破局限型 dominates (4/8): "X inadequate → diagnose → fix." 化繁为简型 secondary (3/8): "partial solutions → unify." For turn-residual gating, 突破局限型 fits. **7/8, high.**
16	
17	## Hooks
18	
19	1. **Concrete failure**: li2024 GPT-3.5 screenshot; xiao2024 PPL 5→5158. Visceral. (2/8, high)
20	2. **Provocative title**: gromov "Unreasonable Ineffectiveness"; sun "Curse of Depth." (2/8, medium)
21	3. **Genealogy**: peng2024 PI→NTK→YaRN evolution. (1/8, medium)
22	
23	## Figure 1
24	
25	**Multi-panel mandatory** (7/8). Dominant type: method_overview (5/8), often double-duty showing method + key result. Best examples: qiu2025 (3-panel: positions + bars + loss curves), sun2025 (architecture + "66% fewer params" annotation). Include quantitative result in Fig 1 — pure method diagrams without numbers are less compelling. **Support**: 7/8. **Confidence**: high.
26	
27	## Result Visualization
28	
29	- **Line plots for trends** (6/8): pruning fraction, context length, training steps, turns. "Flat-then-cliff" (gromov) and "diverging curves" (xiao, han) are visually powerful.
30	- **Heatmaps for attention/similarity** (3/8): before/after attention maps are standard proof for mechanism papers.
31	- **Scatter for trade-offs** (1/8): li2024's Pareto front (stability vs performance) ideal for multi-objective methods.
32	**Support**: 6/8. **Confidence**: high.
33	
34	## Table Design
35	
36	**Row organization**: by_category (5/8) — group methods by type. **Highlight**: bold_best (4/8); dramatic numerical contrast can self-highlight (xiao: 5158 vs 5.40). **Reporting**: single run for pre-training (6/8); mean±std for SFT. **Ablation style**: full_numbers dominates (6/8); include mechanism columns alongside performance (qiu2025: gate-score + M-Act + F-Attn with PPL). **Support**: 6/8. **Confidence**: high.
37	
38	## Figure vs Table Division
39	
40	Trends → figures (gromov: 1 table, all figures — phase transition needs curves). Point comparisons → tables. Standard: main results table + mechanism figures + hyperparameter appendix. **5/8, high.**
41	
42	## Surgical Ablations
43	
44	Most persuasive ablations change exactly one thing to kill one hypothesis: xiao2024's linebreak replacement (sinks are positional, not semantic), qiu2025's Switch-Heads 1-expert reduction (gating, not routing, drives gains), gromov2024's simple-vs-informed pruning (similarity analysis unnecessary after healing). Design each ablation to disprove one alternative explanation. **Support**: 3/8. **Confidence**: high.
45	
46	## Intro Structure
47	
48	4-6 paragraphs typical. Pattern: P1=problem + urgency, P2=existing solutions + their limits, P3=this paper's insight, P4=contributions list (3-4 items, numbered or bulleted), P5=results summary. Contributions framed as discovery+solution pairs work best (sun2025: "identify curse + propose fix"). **Support**: 7/8. **Confidence**: high.

      "content": "---\nname: distilled-writing\ndescription: \"Narrative structure, figure/table design, and visual rhetoric patterns for multi-turn/depth/gating LLM papers — distilled from 8 deep-read papers at ICLR/NeurIPS/ACL/COLM 2024-2025\"\nscope: project\nversion: 1\ntags: [distilled, auto-generated]\ndistilled_from:\n  paper_count: 25\n  paper_keys: [bai2024mt, gromov2024unreasonable, sun2025curse, li2024measuring, qiu2025gated, peng2024yarn, xiao2024efficient, han2024lm, makkuva2025attention, ildiz2024self, chiang2024chatbot, liu2023lost, luo2025adaptive, ko2024found, zheng2023judging, sirdeshmukh2025multichallenge, fan2020reducing, schuster2022confident, wang2018skipnet, press2022train, dubois2024length, bengio2015scheduled, ranzato2016sequence, lamb2016professor, leblond2018searnn]\n  last_distilled: \"2026-05-22T13:00:00Z\"\n---\n\n## Story Types\n\n突破局限型 dominates (4/8): \"X inadequate → diagnose → fix.\" 化繁为简型 secondary (3/8): \"partial solutions → unify.\" For turn-residual gating, 突破局限型 fits. **7/8, high.**\n\n## Hooks\n\n1. **Concrete failure**: li2024 GPT-3.5 screenshot; xiao2024 PPL 5→5158. Visceral. (2/8, high)\n2. **Provocative title**: gromov \"Unreasonable Ineffectiveness\"; sun \"Curse of Depth.\" (2/8, medium)\n3. **Genealogy**: peng2024 PI→NTK→YaRN evolution. (1/8, medium)\n\n## Figure 1\n\n**Multi-panel mandatory** (7/8). Dominant type: method_overview (5/8), often double-duty showing method + key result. Best examples: qiu2025 (3-panel: positions + bars + loss curves), sun2025 (architecture + \"66% fewer params\" annotation). Include quantitative result in Fig 1 — pure method diagrams without numbers are less compelling. **Support**: 7/8. **Confidence**: high.\n\n## Result Visualization\n\n- **Line plots for trends** (6/8): pruning fraction, context length, training steps, turns. \"Flat-then-cliff\" (gromov) and \"diverging curves\" (xiao, han) are visually powerful.\n- **Heatmaps for attention/similarity** (3/8): before/after attention maps are standard proof for mechanism papers.\n- **Scatter for trade-offs** (1/8): li2024's Pareto front (stability vs performance) ideal for multi-objective methods.\n**Support**: 6/8. **Confidence**: high.\n\n## Table Design\n\n**Row organization**: by_category (5/8) — group methods by type. **Highlight**: bold_best (4/8); dramatic numerical contrast can self-highlight (xiao: 5158 vs 5.40). **Reporting**: single run for pre-training (6/8); mean±std for SFT. **Ablation style**: full_numbers dominates (6/8); include mechanism columns alongside performance (qiu2025: gate-score + M-Act + F-Attn with PPL). **Support**: 6/8. **Confidence**: high.\n\n## Figure vs Table Division\n\nTrends → figures (gromov: 1 table, all figures — phase transition needs curves). Point comparisons → tables. Standard: main results table + mechanism figures + hyperparameter appendix. **5/8, high.**\n\n## Surgical Ablations\n\nMost persuasive ablations change exactly one thing to kill one hypothesis: xiao2024's linebreak replacement (sinks are positional, not semantic), qiu2025's Switch-Heads 1-expert reduction (gating, not routing, drives gains), gromov2024's simple-vs-informed pruning (similarity analysis unnecessary after healing). Design each ablation to disprove one alternative explanation. **Support**: 3/8. **Confidence**: high.\n\n## Intro Structure\n\n4-6 paragraphs typical. Pattern: P1=problem + urgency, P2=existing solutions + their limits, P3=this paper's insight, P4=contributions list (3-4 items, numbered or bulleted), P5=results summary. Contributions framed as discovery+solution pairs work best (sun2025: \"identify curse + propose fix\"). **Support**: 7/8. **Confidence**: high.\n",

**3. Intro structure**: Checking the flow — P1 establishes the problem with attention decay and quantifies the urgency through that 70.8% decline metric. P2 layers in existing knowledge about residual streams retaining information (AUC=0.98) while identifying where the mechanism breaks down. The structure continues from there.

**4. PCGrad exclusion + gradient quantification)
- (2) empirical characterization (exposure bias destroys auxiliary objectives)
- (3) inference-time steering (0.064 → 0.727)
- (4) principled negative baseline (7 loss configs, 5-way comparison)

These aren't really paired discoveries and solutions. It's more framework, finding, experiment, baseline. The ideal format would be "identify X + propose Y" for each, but this paper's structure is different—ThreadGate fundamentally fails, so the solution aspect is weaker. The real discoveries are that exposure bias causes the collapse, steering can recover performance, and scheduled sampling helps compress the gap. Maybe the pairing should be: exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary objectives → steering recovers GAR while scheduled sampling narrows the gap, and gating provides zero benefit → five-way controlled comparison validates this. Actually, for a negative result with mechanistic analysis, the current structure makes sense—the contributions don't naturally pair as discovery+solution because the paper's main finding is that the interventions don't work.

Now looking at Figure 1, which shows ThreadGate adding per-layer gating to the residual stream trained with an auxiliary loss targeting goal attention, and then comparing teacher-forced evaluation (where GAR reaches 0.50) against autoregressive generation where it collapses to 0.015–0.025.

Checking the table structure—Table 1 currently has five rows comparing Base, ThreadGate, PCGrad, Uniform Gate, and LoRA-only, but doesn't bold any cells or group by category. Since the point is that all variants perform equally (the negative result), bolding the best doesn't emphasize much, though I could highlight LoRA-only to show it matches everything else. The table could be reorganized by grouping gated variants together.

The dramatic numerical contrast between TF GAR (0.50) and AR GAR (0.012-0.025) creates a 20-33× gap that's inherently striking, but the table formatting doesn't make this pop—the numbers just sit there. Adding a gap column or making the AR column visually prominent would help. Looking at the figures, Fig 2 shows mostly flat or overlapping training curves between ThreadGate and PCGrad, demonstrating that PCGrad doesn't help, while Fig 4's attention heatmap displays per-turn GAR values under TF evaluation.

The fine-tuned variants stay flat near 0.5 while the base model decays by 80%, creating a clear divergence between the two groups. Fig 5 already captures the "flat-then-cliff" nonlinearity pattern between β=2 and β=5. However, there's a potential gap: the paper describes an attention heatmap but doesn't actually show before/after attention patterns comparing pre- and post-training or pre- and post-steering states, which would be more compelling evidence than just the GAR metrics.

Looking at the actual figures and compiling the gaps, I see several formatting opportunities in Table 1 — bolding the LoRA-only results to emphasize how a simple approach matches the complex gated methods, and potentially grouping rows with dividers to separate base from fine-tuned variants or gated from ungated approaches. These are medium-priority improvements that could make the key finding more visually striking.

The paper also lacks the standard before-and-after attention visualizations that typically strengthen mechanistic claims, though generating new figures might not be feasible in the timeframe. There's also a minor naming inconsistency where Figure 4 is labeled as an attention heatmap when it's actually a line plot.

Let me step back and systematically check the ten items the Director asked about — the story type is clearly a breakthrough-overcoming-limitations narrative, and the hook in the intro effectively uses the 70.8% GAR decline as a visceral opening. diverging curves between fine-tuned and base models. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a clear picture of all the figures and pages. Let me compile my assessment:

**1. Story type**: ✅ Clear 突破局限型. ThreadGate proposed → diagnoses why it fails (exposure bias) → provides mitigations (steering, scheduled sampling). Very clear.

**2. Hook**: ✅ Intro P1 has "70.8% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn 1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation." Visceral, concrete failure data. Good hook.


**3. Intro structure**: The opening maps out the problem and urgency well in P1, then P2 blends existing knowledge with our insight rather than separating them cleanly—it mentions residual stream retention and the ThreadGate discovery together instead of keeping the existing limitations distinct from our contribution. Figure 1 breaks up the flow before P3 pivots to ThreadGate's design and the five-way comparison with PCGrad.

P4 summarizes the results, and P5 lists the contributions. Compared to the recommended structure (problem → existing + limits → insight → contributions → results), we've swapped the order of contributions and results, and merged existing knowledge with insight. This actually works well for a negative-result paper where the failure needs explanation before we highlight what was learned.

**4. Contributions format**: The current list (1)-(4) starts with the diagnostic framework using the five-way comparison, then...

Looking at how to reframe these contributions as discovery-plus-solution pairs. The challenge is that this paper's core finding is that proposed fixes don't work — the discovery itself is the failure. I could reframe (2) and (3) together: identifying exposure bias as selectively destroying auxiliary objectives (with the 20-33× gap as evidence) and then showing constructive evidence through inference-time steering, even though the steering doesn't fully solve the problem.

But the current four-item structure is already established and changing it risks pushing past the page limit, so I'll keep this as a minor gap.

Figure 1 is well-designed with a multi-panel layout that shows the observation on the left (GAR decay and the ThreadGate architecture with natural hypothesis) and the results on the right (the stark difference between TF and AR performance, gradient conflict analysis, and how PCGrad and distribution shift explain the mechanism). The figure includes quantitative annotations throughout, making both the method and key findings clear.

Looking at Table 1 on page 6, I notice there's no bold formatting anywhere and no category grouping with separators—all five rows are just listed sequentially. The caption does highlight the main point about convergence under TF, but the table itself doesn't use the formatting conventions that most papers employ, like bolding the best values or grouping rows by category. For a negative result paper where all variants converge, bolding the best doesn't add much since there's no clear winner, though I could still add a separator after the base model to improve readability.

On the dramatic numerical contrast front, Table 1 does show a striking 20-33× difference between the AR GAR-O column (0.012-0.025) and the TF columns (0.50-0.51), but the contrast spans across different column groups rather than sitting within a single column like in other papers. Adding an explicit ratio column showing the TF/AR gap would make that dramatic difference much more visually apparent and compelling.

Now looking at the figures — Figure 2 shows the diverging curves pattern nicely with fine-tuned variants staying flat around 0.5 while the base model decays sharply to 80%, and Figure 3 captures the flat-then-cliff effect with the steering parameter β showing minimal change until the [2,5] range where it jumps dramatically. Figure 4's gate conditioning bar chart works fine for its data type, though it's not a curve. Figure 5's training curves are mostly overlapping, which is less visually striking.

The real gap is the missing attention heatmaps — the paper claims to have them but Figure 4 is actually just a line plot of per-turn GAR. A proper heatmap showing how the base model's attention to goal tokens decays while the fine-tuned model maintains it under teacher forcing but collapses under autoregressive decoding would be compelling mechanistic evidence. The constraint is the 8-page limit with no room to add figures unless we compress existing ones.

Now I'm organizing what needs fixing by feasibility. The quick wins are Table 1 formatting — adding visual grouping between base and fine-tuned sections plus bolding the best values per column. There's also a question of whether adding a TF/AR gap column would strengthen the numerical contrast, though that depends on page space. For the narrative, splitting the second paragraph into distinct sections for existing knowledge versus our contribution would sharpen the framing. The harder calls are whether to restructure how we present contributions and whether generating a new attention heatmap is worth the effort given the tight timeline. impact but may not be feasible):**
5. Missing before/after attention heatmap: Standard for mechanism papers, currently absent. Would require new figure generation + page budget.

**No gap (items that pass):**
- Story type: clear 突破局限型 ✓
- Hook: concrete 70.8% failure data ✓
- Fig 1: multi-panel with quantitative annotations ✓
- Trend plots: flat-then-cliff (steering) + diverging curves (per-turn GAR) ✓

Let me report this to the Director.

Actually, I need to think through feasibility more carefully. The table formatting fix is straightforward LaTeX work. Adding a gap column should fit since Table 1 uses full width, though I'm concerned about the overfull hbox warnings elsewhere. Restructuring the intro would be easy but might damage the rhetorical flow between the residual stream and gating mechanism points, so it's low priority. Reformatting the contributions is too risky after multiple review rounds. The attention heatmap is the real blocker—I'd need to access experiment outputs, write visualization code, run it on the server, and squeeze it into the page budget, so the Director should weigh whether that's worth pursuing.

_source line 3314 · 2026-05-22T17:08:04.183Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HsH2hZvr1tDFTBKwiQs5Gq`</summary>

```text
4	
5	A striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \emph{not} affect. \citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\,=\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.
6	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: identical gate architecture with turn embedding replaced by a still-learnable constant vector, preserving all gate parameters except turn conditioning (content-conditioned but turn-unaware; isolates the contribution of turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. We cannot exclude the possibility that LoRA capacity saturates at rank~16, masking potential gating benefits at higher adapter ranks; however, rank~16 is standard for 7B-scale models and the uniform convergence across four architecturally distinct variants makes capacity saturation an unlikely sole explanation. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations under our evaluation conditions. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode under these conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nAn initial sweep on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier. We note that this steering experiment is diagnostic, not a deployment strategy: it requires explicit identification of goal tokens, which is trivial for system prompts but does not generalize to implicit goals.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), consistent with distribution shift as the primary failure mode under these conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions fail uniformly. We test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR by applying an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nAn initial sweep on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals a sharp nonlinearity between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 with PPL 1.77$\\pm$0.27, confirming the recovery pattern at scale.\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier. We note that this steering experiment is diagnostic, not a deployment strategy: it requires explicit identification of goal tokens, which is trivial for system prompts but does not generalize to implicit goals.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",
