    "oldString": "This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.",
    "newString": "This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention point.\n\nIf the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \\citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \\citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.\n\nA striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \\emph{not} affect. \\citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\\,=\\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n  \\caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}\n  \\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way controlled comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one factor (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.\n\nTraining succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The five-way comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a gap exceeding an order of magnitude that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient direction conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, to our knowledge the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline: seven loss configurations across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:loss-iterations}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}), of which only MSE converges, and a five-way comparison ruling out four alternative training strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RxTfuKuzQo2sNrCmkU4TwG`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause (\S\ref{subsec:steering}). Alternative hypotheses remain open; our evidence is consistent with exposure bias as the dominant factor but does not exclude alternatives. In particular, KV cache drift---the accumulation of model-generated key-value states whose statistics diverge from teacher-forced states---could compound the distributional mismatch beyond what prefix-level exposure bias alone predicts. Disentangling prefix-level and cache-level contributions requires interventions on cached representations, which we leave to future work. Split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring} and Rhea \citep{hong2025rhea} complement $\beta$-steering by restructuring attention computation at the architectural level, but cannot resolve exposure bias in auxiliary losses requiring retraining.
4	
5	\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. AR GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the ${\sim}$31$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\ 1.36), consistent with exposure bias as a primary driver. GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\to$0.353). On the $N$\,=\,50 evaluation, $\beta$\,=\,5 incurs 24\% PPL cost (1.43\,$\to$\,1.77); $\beta$\,=\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (+9\%); scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining. A from-scratch second seed yields AR GAR\,=\,0.418 (gap 1.20$\times$), suggesting early scheduled sampling exposure may further compress the gap; systematic ablation is deferred.
6	
7	Exploratory instruction retention analysis (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ir}) shows +23.8\,pp content compliance at $\beta$\,=\,3, though statistical significance is marginal (McNemar $p$\,=\,0.077).
8	
9	TRC analysis (\S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without degrading representations. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.
10	
```

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	ThreadGate modulates the residual stream with per-layer gated MLP networks and an auxiliary GAR loss. Training succeeds (TF GAR\,=\,0.50), but autoregressive GAR collapses by more than an order of magnitude (exposure bias), confirmed on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\times$ by median). Inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5, GAR 0.727) and scheduled sampling (gap ${\sim}$31$\times$$\to$1.57$\times$, no PPL cost, single seed) both point to attention misallocation---not representation degradation---as a proximal cause consistent with our evidence.
4

Disentangling prefix-level and cache-level contributions requires interventions on cached representations, which we leave to future work.",
    "newString": "In particular, KV cache drift---accumulation of model-generated key-value states diverging from teacher-forced statistics---could compound the mismatch; disentangling prefix-level and cache-level contributions is left to future work.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). Alternative hypotheses remain open; our evidence is consistent with exposure bias as the dominant factor but does not exclude alternatives. In particular, KV cache drift---the accumulation of model-generated key-value states whose statistics diverge from teacher-forced states---could compound the distributional mismatch beyond what prefix-level exposure bias alone predicts. Disentangling prefix-level and cache-level contributions requires interventions on cached representations, which we leave to future work. Split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} and Rhea \\citep{hong2025rhea} complement $\\beta$-steering by restructuring attention computation at the architectural level, but cannot resolve exposure bias in auxiliary losses requiring retraining.\n\n\\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. AR GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\\ 1.36), consistent with exposure bias as a primary driver. GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\\to$0.353). On the $N$\\,=\\,50 evaluation, $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 incurs 24\\% PPL cost (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.77); $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (+9\\%); scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining. A from-scratch second seed yields AR GAR\\,=\\,0.418 (gap 1.20$\\times$), suggesting early scheduled sampling exposure may further compress the gap; systematic ablation is deferred.\n\nExploratory instruction retention analysis (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}) shows +23.8\\,pp content compliance at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, though statistical significance is marginal (McNemar $p$\\,=\\,0.077).\n\nTRC analysis (\\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without degrading representations. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.\n",

The Experiments section is where most of the bloat accumulated. The LoRA saturation note is running long at two and a half lines—I can condense it by removing the justification about rank-16 being standard and just emphasizing that the convergence across different architectures makes saturation unlikely as a sole explanation. The steering circularity point also needs tightening; it's getting into deployment considerations that might be better left implicit or moved to a footnote.

For the Related Work section, that Gromov citation is doing a lot of work but the connection to my findings could be stated more directly without the full setup about depth interventions being surprisingly effective.
```

</details>
